  136. The correct answer is B (Approximately 30 to 60 minutes after collection, depending on tube type and clot activator): Most serum tubes require a clotting period of roughly 30 to 60 minutes, depending on whether a clot activator is present, before centrifugation to ensure a complete and stable clot has formed. Spinning immediately, before clotting is complete, risks fibrin strands and an incomplete separation that can interfere with testing. Waiting significantly longer than necessary, such as 6 hours, can allow analyte changes from prolonged cell contact with serum. Following the recommended clotting window balances complete clot formation against unnecessary analyte degradation.
  137. The correct answer is C (Prolonged contact with cells can cause certain analytes to shift due to ongoing cellular metabolism and diffusion.): Even after centrifugation, prolonged contact between serum or plasma and the cellular layer can allow substances to diffuse across the barrier or ongoing metabolic activity in remaining cells to alter analyte concentrations, such as potassium or glucose. This means composition can still change after the spin if separation and removal are delayed too long. This concern applies broadly across chemistry testing, not exclusively to coagulation assays. Prompt separation after centrifugation helps lock in an accurate representation of the analyte levels at the time of collection.
  138. The correct answer is B (The green heparin tube, since it contains an anticoagulant rather than a clot activator.): Plasma tubes like the green heparin tube shown contain an anticoagulant that keeps the specimen from clotting at all, allowing it to be centrifuged immediately rather than waiting through the clotting period required for the red/gold SST tube pictured beside it. The light blue citrate tube is also anticoagulated, but it is reserved for coagulation testing rather than routine chemistry centrifugation and is not the tube being asked about here relative to a clotting wait. Claiming the SST tube's clot activator works faster than an anticoagulant reverses the actual relationship between the two additive types. Recognizing which tube shown skips the clotting period explains why a green tube is often chosen for STAT chemistry requests.
  139. The correct answer is A (Running control materials with known target values and confirming results fall within the acceptable range.): Quality control materials with established target ranges must be run and verified as acceptable before patient testing begins, confirming the analyzer is performing accurately and precisely that day. Using a patient sample instead of a validated control material does not provide the same assurance since the true value is unknown. Skipping quality control based on the analyzer's apparent normal appearance ignores subtle performance drift that only formal QC can detect. Daily QC verification, not just periodic calibration, is a standard and required part of processing patient samples.
  140. The correct answer is A (Investigate and resolve the issue before releasing any patient results from that run.): An out-of-range result from the control tube shown signals a potential problem with the analyzer, reagents, or process that could produce inaccurate patient results, so the issue must be investigated and resolved before any results from that run are released. Releasing results despite a known QC failure risks reporting inaccurate data that could affect patient care decisions. Dismissing a single failure without investigation ignores a real signal that something may be wrong. Proper corrective action and documentation are required whenever quality control falls outside acceptable limits.
  141. The correct answer is B (Improper processing can introduce pre-analytical errors that analyzer QC cannot detect.): Analyzer quality control checks the instrument's own performance but cannot detect a problem like the incomplete clot formation and residual fibrin shown, which can still produce an inaccurate result even on a perfectly functioning analyzer. This is why correct specimen processing is considered an essential pre-analytical quality control measure in its own right. Believing that analyzer QC alone covers all sources of error overlooks this important distinction. Recognizing pre-analytical quality control as a distinct category helps the MLA/T understand their direct role in overall result accuracy.
  142. The correct answer is C (PCR can amplify even trace amounts of contaminating genetic material, producing a false-positive result.): PCR is designed to exponentially amplify even minute quantities of target genetic material, which means even a tiny amount of contaminating DNA or RNA from another specimen can be amplified and reported as a false positive. This extreme sensitivity is exactly why strict technique, dedicated work areas, and careful handling are emphasized for molecular sample processing. The concern is distinct from, and in some ways greater than, contamination concerns in traditional culture-based testing. Understanding this sensitivity underscores why molecular processing protocols are especially strict about preventing cross-contamination.
  143. The correct answer is B (Lipemia can interfere with some assays, and the finding should be noted and referred to an MLT.): Lipemia can physically interfere with certain photometric or immunologic assays, producing inaccurate results or requiring special handling before testing can proceed. Noting the finding and referring it to an MLT ensures the appropriate corrective action, such as ultracentrifugation or an alternate method, is applied by someone with the authority to make that determination. Diluting the specimen with saline is not an appropriate independent action for the MLA/T to take without proper authorization. Recognizing and reporting this pre-analytical interference, rather than ignoring it or acting unilaterally, is the correct scope of the MLA/T's role.
  144. The correct answer is B (Visible hemolysis, icterus, or turbidity that could interfere with accurate results.): Hemolysis, icterus, and turbidity are all recognized visible indicators that can interfere with many laboratory assays and should prompt the MLA/T to flag the specimen for further review by an MLT rather than proceeding automatically. A slightly cool tube temperature, morning versus afternoon collection, or requisition ink color are not recognized interference indicators requiring this kind of escalation. Correctly identifying which visible characteristics matter clinically, rather than flagging irrelevant details, keeps the review process efficient and focused. This distinction is part of understanding the scope of the MLA/T role in identifying potential deficiencies.
  145. The correct answer is D (Refer the sequencing error and its potential impact on the results to an MLT.): Recognizing that the tubes shown were collected out of order, with the green tube ahead of the light blue tube, is within the MLA/T's role, but determining whether that specific error is significant enough to affect the coagulation result requires the broader scope of practice held by an MLT. Deciding independently that the error shown is insignificant oversteps the MLA/T's defined authority. Claiming MLA/Ts cannot notice sequencing issues at all misunderstands their actual responsibility to flag concerns like the one pictured. Prompt referral, rather than delaying until a shift change, ensures the coagulation result is evaluated before it reaches the chart.
  146. The correct answer is D (Test request, requisition generation, specimen collection, specimen receipt, result entry, and transfer to the EMR.): The LIS is designed to track a logical sequence beginning with the provider's test request, followed by requisition generation, specimen collection, receipt in the laboratory, testing and result entry, and finally transfer of the finalized result into the patient's EMR. Reversing this order, such as specimen receipt happening before the request, does not reflect how the actual clinical and laboratory workflow proceeds. The EMR receives information from the LIS rather than independently generating the original test request. Understanding this correct sequence helps the MLA/T recognize where in the process any given specimen or order currently stands.
  147. The correct answer is D (Investigate and resolve the discrepancy before allowing the specimen to proceed through testing.): A mismatch between the LIS accession number and the physical tube label represents a serious identification discrepancy that must be investigated and resolved before testing proceeds, since either error could result in a result being linked to the wrong specimen or patient. Simply assuming either the LIS or the label is automatically correct without investigation risks compounding the original error. Proceeding with testing despite the unresolved mismatch ignores a fundamental threat to result accuracy and traceability. Careful investigation protects against a downstream misidentified result.
  148. The correct answer is A (Managing order entry, specimen tracking, quality control data, and interfacing with instruments and the EMR.): A modern LIS manages the full laboratory workflow, including order entry, specimen tracking, instrument interfacing, and quality control data management, in addition to storing final results and passing them to the EMR. Describing it as a passive filing cabinet understates its active, integrated role throughout the testing process. Physical specimen labeling remains essential regardless of how sophisticated the LIS is, since the physical specimen must still be correctly matched to its digital record. While the LIS does support billing functions, this is only one part of its much broader role in laboratory operations.
  149. The correct answer is C (It helps identify when results are not transferring correctly, preventing a delay or loss of patient data): Recognizing signs of an interface failure, such as results not appearing where expected, allows the MLA/T to flag the issue promptly, which can prevent a significant delay or even loss of important patient result data. While IT staff typically resolve the underlying technical problem, front-line recognition by laboratory staff is often what triggers that response in the first place. Interface failures, when they do occur, can meaningfully affect turnaround time and patient care if unnoticed. This basic awareness is a practical skill that supports the broader goal of accurate and timely result reporting.
  150. The correct answer is D (Refer the abnormal or critical result to an MLT promptly according to facility protocol.): Because the tubes pictured were collected in the correct standard sequence, the flagged potassium value from the green tube cannot be dismissed as a draw-order artifact, which makes prompt referral to an MLT for clinical evaluation the appropriate next step. Releasing the result without review skips the safety step designed to catch genuinely critical values before they reach the chart. Discarding the specimen based on an assumed but unconfirmed draw-order problem is not within the MLA/T's authority and could suppress a real critical finding. Timely referral, rather than delay until a shift change, is essential given the urgency implied by a critical potassium value from a properly sequenced tube.
  151. The correct answer is C (Critical values represent results that could indicate an immediate, potentially life-threatening situation for the patient): Critical values are specifically defined because they represent results significantly outside the normal or even standard abnormal range, indicating a situation that could be immediately life-threatening if not addressed quickly. This distinct notification requirement exists to ensure rapid communication reaches the clinical team without delay, unlike routine abnormal results that follow standard reporting timelines. The critical value concept applies broadly across many testing disciplines, including chemistry, hematology, and microbiology, not just one area. Understanding why this separate category exists helps clarify the urgency behind the required notification process.
  152. The correct answer is A (Immediately bring the flagged critical result to the attention of the responsible MLT.): Bringing a critical result to the immediate attention of the responsible MLT ensures it receives timely, appropriate clinical judgment and follow-up per protocol, reflecting the correct scope of the MLA/T role. Assuming an error and quietly requesting a redraw without informing anyone bypasses the required escalation process for a potentially urgent finding. Simply entering the result and moving on treats a critical value the same as a routine one, missing the urgency it represents. Passive reliance on the physician noticing during rounds could introduce a dangerous delay in addressing a life-threatening finding.
  153. The correct answer is A (Decline to release any results, since the caller is not an authorized recipient of that information.): Patient confidentiality regulations restrict result disclosure to authorized individuals, typically the patient themselves or specifically designated care providers, and a friend calling on their own does not meet this standard regardless of how concerned they sound. Basic verification questions or knowing a piece of demographic information like date of birth do not establish legal authorization to receive protected health information. Declining to release any results to an unauthorized caller is the appropriate and legally required response. This protects the patient's confidentiality rights regardless of the caller's intentions.
  154. The correct answer is A (Overheard conversations in public spaces can violate patient confidentiality even if no names are directly stated.): Even without stating a patient's full name, enough identifying detail can sometimes be overheard in a public conversation to allow someone to infer the patient's identity, which constitutes a confidentiality breach. Confidentiality regulations cover verbal disclosures just as much as written records, not just formal documentation. Believing public conversations are exempt from these regulations misunderstands the scope of patient privacy protections. Being mindful of where and how patient information is discussed is a practical and ongoing professional responsibility.
  155. The correct answer is D (Refuse the request, since accessing records without a legitimate care-related reason violates confidentiality regulations.): Accessing patient records requires a legitimate, care-related reason, and simple curiosity or personal relationship with the patient does not meet this standard, even for another laboratory employee. Working within the same laboratory does not automatically grant a legitimate need to access every patient's record. Partially complying by looking up the information but withholding specific numbers still constitutes an unauthorized access violation. A promise of confidentiality from the requesting coworker does not make the underlying unauthorized access acceptable, and the request should be refused.
  156. The correct answer is D (These requirements ensure results are reviewed, accurate, and legally defensible before reaching the patient's chart.): Following defined procedures and regulatory requirements ensures that every recorded result passes through the proper review and verification steps before becoming part of the patient's official record, protecting both accuracy and legal defensibility. Treating these procedures as optional undermines the entire purpose of having a structured, verifiable reporting process. Legal and regulatory requirements for proper handling apply to all results, not exclusively abnormal ones. These procedures exist to protect patient safety and reporting integrity, not simply to add unnecessary delay.
  157. The correct answer is D (A physician could make an incorrect treatment decision, and the facility could face legal liability.): An inaccurate result reaching the chart can directly influence a physician's treatment decisions, potentially leading to inappropriate care, and can also expose the facility to legal liability if the error is traced back to a preventable handling mistake. Dismissing the consequence as insignificant ignores the real potential for patient harm from acting on incorrect data. This concern is about direct clinical impact, not merely administrative or billing delays. Recognizing these serious potential consequences reinforces why careful specimen handling and result verification matter at every step.
  158. The correct answer is C (It reinforces the importance of accuracy and diligence at every step the MLA/T is directly responsible for): Understanding the downstream consequences of an inaccurate result, even though the MLA/T does not interpret results themselves, reinforces why careful attention to identification, collection, and handling matters at every step within their own defined scope. Dismissing this understanding as irrelevant overlooks the direct connection between the MLA/T's daily tasks and eventual patient outcomes. While MLTs bear responsibility for interpretation, MLA/Ts are equally responsible for the accuracy of the steps that precede it. This awareness applies to everyday practice, not only to rare legal proceedings.
  159. The correct answer is A (Retention supports legal defensibility, quality audits, and continuity of patient care records over time.): Legally defined retention periods ensure that documentation remains available for legal defense, quality audits, accreditation review, and ongoing patient care continuity long after a specific test has been completed and reported. Treating retention periods as arbitrary or a matter of personal facility preference misunderstands that they are typically set by regulatory or accreditation bodies. Discarding documents immediately after reporting would eliminate the ability to investigate a later quality concern or legal question. These retention requirements apply to both paper and electronic records, since both forms of documentation carry the same legal and clinical significance.
  160. The correct answer is D (Secure destruction, such as shredding, to prevent unauthorized access to the confidential information.): Documents containing patient identifiable information must be securely destroyed, typically through shredding or an equivalent method, to prevent any unauthorized access to confidential health information even after the retention period ends. Placing them in regular recycling without secure destruction risks a serious confidentiality breach. Leaving such documents unsecured, even temporarily, creates an unnecessary and avoidable risk of exposure. Recognizing that disposal itself requires the same careful confidentiality standard as active record-keeping is an important extension of the retention requirement.
  161. The correct answer is D (WHMIS 2015, which sets national hazard classification, labelling, and safety data sheet requirements for suppliers.): this system sets national rules for classifying hazards and requires suppliers to provide labels and safety data sheets for hazardous products used at work. The Transportation of Dangerous Goods Act instead governs shipping, not on-site labelling. The Nuclear Safety Act and Human Pathogens Act cover narrower, specific hazard types. WHMIS 2015 is the broad chemical hazard communication framework laboratory staff rely on daily.
  162. The correct answer is D (The Transportation of Dangerous Goods Act and Regulations, which govern classification and shipping documentation.): this federal law governs the classification, packaging, labelling, and documentation required to legally ship dangerous goods, including biological substances, by any mode of transport. The Occupational Health and Safety Act covers workplace conditions rather than shipping. Hazardous Products Regulations govern supplier labelling for on-site use, not transport documentation. Nuclear safety legislation applies only to radioactive materials, not routine specimen shipments.
  163. The correct answer is B (The classification criteria and mandatory disclosure requirements for hazardous products supplied to a workplace.): the Hazardous Products Regulations set out the specific technical criteria suppliers must use to classify hazards and prepare compliant labels and safety data sheets under WHMIS 2015. This has nothing to do with shipping routes, staffing, or licensing. The regulations exist specifically to standardize how hazard information reaches the workplace. Laboratory staff depend on this consistency to correctly interpret labels.
  164. The correct answer is C (The Human Pathogens and Toxins Act, which regulates possession, handling, and security of pathogens.): this federal act specifically regulates the safe handling, storage, and security of human pathogens and toxins in Canadian facilities, including diagnostic laboratories. The Occupational Health and Safety Act is broader workplace legislation, not pathogen-specific. Hazardous Products Regulations address chemical labelling, not biological agents. The Transportation of Dangerous Goods Act governs shipping rather than in-facility handling and security.
  165. The correct answer is C (The Nuclear Safety and Control Act, which regulates nuclear substances and radioactive materials.): this federal legislation regulates the possession, use, and storage of nuclear substances and radioactive materials, including those found in nuclear medicine departments. WHMIS 2015 addresses chemical hazards, not radiation. The Human Pathogens and Toxins Act covers biological agents, not radioactive isotopes. The Occupational Health and Safety Act is general workplace legislation without radiation-specific licensing authority.
  166. The correct answer is C (It establishes shared employer and worker duties intended to prevent workplace injury and illness.): Occupational Health and Safety legislation defines the responsibilities of both employers and workers to identify hazards, provide training, and maintain a safe environment. It has no role in setting reimbursement rates or reference ranges. It also does not regulate equipment exports. Its core function is prevention of workplace injury and illness through shared responsibility.
  167. The correct answer is B (To provide detailed information about the chemical's hazards, safe handling, and emergency response measures.): a Safety Data Sheet is a standardized sixteen-section document that details a chemical's hazards, safe handling, storage, and emergency response information. It is not a pricing document or a quality control record. Its purpose is purely safety and hazard communication. Laboratory staff should consult it before working with an unfamiliar chemical.
  168. The correct answer is B (The Transportation of Dangerous Goods Act and Regulations, which govern packaging and shipping documentation.): shipping a leaking or damaged specimen container by courier falls under transportation regulations that govern packaging, labelling, and documentation of dangerous goods in transit. The Human Pathogens and Toxins Act covers in-facility possession and security rather than shipping mechanics. The Occupational Health and Safety Act addresses workplace conditions, not courier transport. WHMIS 2015 governs supplier labelling for workplace chemicals, not specimen shipping.
  169. The correct answer is C (Workplace Hazardous Materials Information System, the correct national hazard communication and labelling framework.): WHMIS stands for this exact system, which standardizes hazard classification, labelling, and safety data sheets for hazardous products in Canadian workplaces. The other options are invented phrases that do not correspond to the real acronym. Knowing the correct expansion helps staff recognize official documentation and training references. WHMIS 2015 aligns Canada's system with the internationally harmonized approach.
  170. The correct answer is C (The right to refuse work reasonably believed to be dangerous, under legislated defined conditions.): most provincial Occupational Health and Safety legislation grants workers this right when they have reasonable grounds to believe a task is dangerous. Scheduling and test selection are administrative matters unrelated to this legal protection. This right exists specifically to prevent forced exposure to unaddressed hazards. Employers must follow a defined investigation process once a refusal is made.
  171. The correct answer is B (All blood and body fluids are treated as potentially infectious regardless of documented patient history.): standard precautions assume every specimen could carry an infectious agent, since infection status is often unknown or undisclosed. This means gloves and hand hygiene apply to all patients, not just those flagged as infected. Gloves never replace hand hygiene, since gloves can develop small unseen defects. This universal approach is the foundation of laboratory infection control.
  172. The correct answer is B (Keep the open container away from open flames, sparks, and other ignition sources nearby.): the flame pictogram identifies xylene as a flammable liquid, so it must be kept clear of any ignition source and used away from heat. Storing it near biohazard specimens does not address its fire risk and mixes unrelated hazard categories. A dust mask does not protect against flammable vapor ignition risk. Municipal waste disposal is inappropriate for a flammable chemical reagent and violates hazardous waste rules.
  173. Discontinuing hand hygiene or removing gloves early would increase risk, not reduce it. Storing a specimen at room temperature is unrelated to transmission-based precautions. Additional precautions exist specifically to address transmission routes routine practices alone do not fully cover.
  174. The correct answer is D (The rule against eating or drinking in any area where specimens are actively handled.): eating at a bench where specimens are processed risks ingestion of contaminants and is a fundamental safe laboratory practice violation. Centrifuge speed, lab coat laundering, and staffing rules are separate policies unrelated to this scenario. Food and drink are generally restricted to designated break areas away from specimen handling. This rule protects staff from accidental exposure through hand-to-mouth contact.
  175. The correct answer is B (Report the hazard and tag the equipment out of service according to institutional protocol): a damaged electrical cord is a physical hazard that must be reported and the equipment removed from service until repaired. Continuing to use damaged equipment or applying tape as a fix increases the risk of shock or fire. Leaving it for another shift delays hazard correction and increases exposure risk. Prompt reporting and tagging protects everyone who might otherwise use the equipment.
  176. The correct answer is B (Every single patient encounter throughout the laboratory, regardless of suspected infection status at all.): routine practices apply universally because infectious status is frequently unknown at the point of specimen collection or processing. Restricting them to specific units or age groups would leave gaps in protection. This universal application is what distinguishes routine practices from targeted additional precautions. Consistency across every encounter is central to effective infection control.
  177. The correct answer is D (Recapping used needles by hand significantly increases the risk of an accidental needlestick injury occurring.): recapping by hand requires guiding a sharp needle back into a narrow opening, which frequently results in an accidental puncture. It has no bearing on specimen accuracy, WHMIS labelling, or magnetism. Safe alternatives include one-handed scoop techniques or, preferably, immediate disposal without recapping. This is one of the most well documented causes of preventable sharps injuries.
  178. The correct answer is D (Gown first, then mask, then eye protection, and finally gloves pulled on last of all.): the standard donning sequence puts gloves on last so they cover the gown cuffs and remain the outermost, most frequently contaminated layer. Donning gloves first would leave the wrists exposed once the gown sleeves are pulled on. This sequence minimizes the chance of contaminating skin or clothing while dressing. Following a consistent order builds a safe, repeatable habit.
  179. The correct answer is A (The gloves, since they are typically the most heavily contaminated item worn during the task): gloves are typically the most heavily contaminated item and are removed first to prevent spreading contamination to other PPE or skin during the doffing process. Removing a mask or gown before gloves risks transferring contamination to the face or clothing. Eye protection is generally removed after gloves and before the mask in most sequences. Hand hygiene should follow glove removal before continuing to remove remaining items.
  180. The correct answer is C (Roll the gown inward so the contaminated outer surface faces inside, avoiding self-contamination entirely.): this technique keeps the potentially contaminated outer surface contained and away from skin and clothing during removal. Pulling it over the head risks contact with the face. Shaking the gown can aerosolize contaminants into the air. Gowns used with contaminated specimens are single use and should never be folded for reuse.
  181. The correct answer is D (Remove and change gloves between patients to prevent cross-contamination between specimens and individuals.): the biohazard pictogram warns that these tubes may contain infectious material, so gloves must be changed between patients to stop pathogens transferring from one specimen or person to the next. Reusing gloves because they look clean ignores that contamination is often invisible. Wiping gloves with a towel does not remove microscopic biological contamination. Wearing the same gloves all shift directly increases cross-contamination risk between patients.
  182. The correct answer is B (Safety goggles or a face shield that specifically provides protection against fluid splash exposure): these provide a sealed or wide barrier against fluid splashes reaching the eyes, unlike regular eyeglasses or sunglasses. Reading glasses and sunglasses lack the side coverage needed to block splash exposure. Skipping eye protection because gloves are worn ignores the specific splash risk to the eyes. Proper splash protection is required whenever an open tube or specimen could spray or splatter.
  183. The correct answer is D (Remove the torn glove, perform hand hygiene, and don a completely new pair of gloves.): torn gloves no longer provide a reliable barrier, so they must be replaced immediately along with hand hygiene in between. Continuing to work with damaged gloves risks direct skin exposure to contaminants. Taping a torn glove does not restore its protective barrier. Finishing the current specimen before switching gloves unnecessarily prolongs the exposure risk.
  184. The correct answer is D (A laboratory coat or gown creates a protective barrier that shields skin and clothing from contamination.): a lab coat or gown acts as a protective barrier against splashes, spills, and contact with specimens during routine work. It is not intended to signal seniority or improve grip on glassware. Dress code alone does not explain its safety function. This barrier role is a core part of standard precautions in the laboratory.
  185. The correct answer is B (The overall risk of an accidental needlestick injury occurring immediately after specimen collection.): safety needle devices include mechanisms such as retractable or shielded needles specifically engineered to reduce the chance of an accidental stick after use. They do not affect specimen quality, labelling accuracy, or chemical exposure. Their design directly targets the highest-risk moment, which is immediately after the needle is withdrawn. This engineering control is a key part of sharps injury prevention programs.
  186. The correct answer is A (Place the used needle directly into an approved, puncture-resistant sharps container right away.): used needles must go straight into a puncture-resistant, labelled sharps container immediately after use to prevent injury to anyone handling waste later. General waste bins and plastic bags offer no puncture protection. Leaving a used needle on a tray creates an unnecessary injury risk to the next person nearby. Immediate, direct disposal is the standard safe practice for all sharps.
  187. The correct answer is A (Remove the container from service immediately and replace it with a new one per protocol.): once a sharps container reaches its indicated fill line, it must be taken out of service and replaced to prevent overfilling, which increases puncture risk during handling. Pushing sharps down to create space is unsafe and can cause injury. Continuing to use an overfull container risks items protruding or spilling. Manual emptying into regular waste is never appropriate for sharps.
  188. The correct answer is C (An acid bottle carrier contains spills and protects the carrier if the bottle is dropped.): acid bottle carriers are designed with a secure grip and containment lip so that a dropped or cracked bottle does not spill acid onto the floor or the carrier's hands. They do not regulate temperature, provide labelling, or measure volume. Their sole function is safe, secure transport of corrosive liquids. Using a carrier significantly reduces the risk of a serious acid exposure incident.
  189. The correct answer is A (A fume hood filters and exhausts hazardous chemical vapours away from the worker's breathing zone.): a fume hood draws air containing chemical vapours away from the worker and typically exhausts it safely outside or through filtration. It is not designed for sterilization, flammable storage, or cooling. Its core function is inhalation hazard control during chemical work. Using it correctly requires keeping the sash at the recommended height for proper airflow.
  190. The correct answer is A (A biological safety cabinet, engineered to protect the worker, sample, and environment together.): this cabinet uses HEPA-filtered airflow specifically engineered to protect the worker, the specimen, and the surrounding environment during aerosol-generating procedures with potentially infectious material. A fume hood protects against chemical vapours but not biological aerosols in the same way. Flammable cabinets and acid carriers serve entirely different chemical storage and transport purposes. Biological safety cabinets are essential wherever infectious aerosols may be generated.
  191. The correct answer is C (A spring-loaded, self-closing lid combined with a built-in flame arrestor mechanism inside.): safety cans are engineered with these features to automatically seal the container and prevent flame from reaching the flammable liquid inside if ignition occurs nearby. A clear glass body or open spout would actually increase fire risk. A heating element would be dangerous and defeats the purpose of safe storage. These design features are what distinguish an approved safety can from an ordinary container.
  192. The correct answer is D (The cabinet primarily exists to isolate and protect flammable chemicals from ignition sources and fire spread.): a dedicated flammable storage cabinet is built with fire-resistant construction to contain vapours and delay ignition in the event of a nearby fire. It is not designed for refrigeration or general waste storage. Its purpose is purely fire prevention and containment for flammable liquids. Using it correctly also involves proper labelling and not overloading it with incompatible materials.
  193. The correct answer is B (An unsecured cylinder can become a dangerous projectile if the valve becomes damaged suddenly.): if a cylinder falls and its valve breaks off, the rapid pressurized gas release can propel the cylinder with enough force to cause serious injury or death. Speed and warranty concerns are not the safety rationale behind cart use. Carts are required for both full and empty cylinders since even an empty cylinder retains some residual pressure risk. Securing cylinders on a proper cart is a critical compressed gas safety practice.
  194. The correct answer is D (Securing the valve cap and chaining the cylinder upright on an approved gas cart.): capping the valve protects it from damage during transport, and chaining the cylinder upright on a cart prevents tipping and uncontrolled gas release. Leaving the regulator attached with the valve open during transport is unsafe. Laying the cylinder flat does not provide adequate protection against rolling or valve damage. Removing labels would also violate WHMIS and transport labelling requirements.
  195. The correct answer is D (The eyewash station located at the designated emergency station within the department.): an eyewash station is specifically designed to flush the eyes with a continuous stream of water immediately following a chemical splash to reduce tissue damage. A fire extinguisher and spill kit address different types of emergencies unrelated to direct eye exposure. A first aid kit's bandages do not address the ongoing chemical exposure to the eye. Immediate eyewash use is critical to limiting injury severity.
  196. The correct answer is D (Flushing continuously for approximately fifteen to twenty minutes is generally recommended practice.): continuous flushing for this duration is generally recommended to thoroughly dilute and remove the chemical from the exposed area and reduce tissue damage. Very short flushing times of only a few seconds are inadequate for meaningful decontamination. Stopping once visible residue disappears may leave residual chemical still causing harm. Following institutional protocol for exact timing is important since some chemicals may require longer flushing.
  197. The correct answer is C (Absorbent material, appropriate neutralizing agents, and dedicated personal protective equipment for the responder.): a spill kit is stocked with these components so a spill can be safely contained, neutralized if appropriate, and cleaned up while protecting the responder. A properly stocked spill kit enables a rapid, safe, and complete response.
  198.
