Placing it directly beside other flammable solvents raises rather than lowers that risk. Biohazard waste containment has nothing to do with this chemical's fire hazard. The two pictograms do not cancel each other out, they describe two separate hazards that compound one another.
  199. The correct answer is D (Smothering a small fire or wrapping a person whose clothing has caught fire.): a fire blanket cuts off the oxygen supply to a small fire or to burning clothing, helping extinguish flames quickly. It is not intended for chemical spill cleanup, cryogenic insulation, or glass disposal. Its specific design makes it ideal for this dual emergency use. Staff should know its storage location as part of basic fire safety training.
  200. The correct answer is B (Pull, Aim, Squeeze, Sweep, describing the standard operating sequence for a fire extinguisher.): this acronym describes the standard technique for operating a fire extinguisher, pulling the pin, aiming at the base of the fire, squeezing the handle, and sweeping side to side. The other option sets are not recognized standard fire extinguisher acronyms. Learning and practicing this sequence helps ensure an effective response during an actual fire emergency. PASS is widely taught in fire safety training across many industries.
  201. The correct answer is B (A minor cut or small injury requiring only immediate, basic first aid care on site.): a first aid kit is stocked for minor injuries that can be managed on site with basic supplies like bandages and antiseptic. Large chemical spills and radioactive contamination require dedicated spill kits and decontamination procedures, not a first aid kit. Fire alarm drills are unrelated to first aid supplies entirely. The kit's scope is limited to minor, non-emergency injury care.
  202. The correct answer is B (Agents posing only minimal potential hazard to laboratory personnel and to the surrounding environment.): containment level 1 is appropriate for low-risk agents that are not generally associated with disease in healthy adults. Highly transmissible, high-risk agents require higher containment levels with more stringent controls. Radioactive isotopes and frozen tissue are not the defining criteria for biosafety containment levels. Level 1 represents the baseline standard microbiological practices used in most routine laboratory work.
  203. The correct answer is D (Restricted access controls along with mandatory use of a biological safety cabinet for aerosols.): level 2 adds these controls because it handles moderate-risk agents capable of causing disease through exposure or ingestion. Saying no additional measures are needed contradicts the entire purpose of tiered containment levels. Eliminating PPE would increase risk rather than manage it appropriately. Room temperature storage of specimens is unrelated to containment level requirements.
  204. The correct answer is A (Inside a certified biological safety cabinet, which contains any aerosols that are generated): the biohazard pictogram indicates the specimen may harbor an infectious organism, so any aerosol-generating step must be performed inside a biological safety cabinet to contain the aerosol and protect the worker. Performing this on the open bench exposes staff and the surrounding area unnecessarily. A chemical fume hood is built for vapors, not infectious aerosols, so it is not an appropriate substitute. The break room is not an approved specimen handling area under any circumstance.
  205. The correct answer is D (Using a leak-proof secondary container that is clearly labelled with the biohazard symbol.): this practice contains any accidental leak during transport and clearly communicates the biological hazard to anyone handling the container. Carrying an open tube by hand or wrapping it loosely in paper provides no real containment. A lab coat pocket is not a secure or leak-proof transport method. Secondary containment is a standard expectation whenever specimens move between areas.
  206. The correct answer is B (Flammable materials, which can easily ignite or are self-reactive under certain defined conditions.): the flame pictogram specifically warns that a substance can easily ignite or is self-reactive under certain conditions. Corrosive hazards use a different pictogram showing damage to a hand and surface. Biohazardous materials use a distinct biohazard symbol, and long-term health hazards use a different pictogram entirely. Recognizing each pictogram correctly is essential for safe chemical handling.
  207. The correct answer is A (The material can cause severe skin burns or eye damage, or corrode metal on contact): the corrosion pictogram visually depicts this exact hazard through images of damage to a hand, a surface, and a test tube. It has no connection to explosive pressure, biological infection, or minor environmental irritation, which are represented by entirely different pictograms. Recognizing this symbol prompts appropriate PPE such as gloves and eye protection. Corrosive chemicals require careful handling procedures outlined in their safety data sheet.
  208. The correct answer is B (Gases under pressure.): the gas cylinder pictogram specifically identifies this pressure-related hazard category. It does not represent corrosive, toxic, or explosive hazard classes, which each have their own distinct pictograms. Recognizing this symbol reminds staff to handle cylinders with appropriate securing and transport precautions. Pressure hazards require different handling than liquid or solid hazardous materials.
  209. The correct answer is C (The exclamation mark pictogram, generally warning of irritation or milder respiratory tract effects): this symbol generally warns of less severe but still important hazards such as skin or eye irritation, respiratory irritation, or drowsiness. The exploding bomb and skull and crossbones pictograms represent more severe hazards like explosivity or acute toxicity. The biohazard pictogram addresses infectious risk rather than chemical irritation. Distinguishing hazard severity through pictograms helps workers apply proportionate precautions.
  210. The correct answer is A (It indicates acute toxicity that can cause serious injury or death even in small amounts): the skull and crossbones pictogram indicates a significantly higher hazard level than the exclamation mark, which covers milder irritation effects. Saying it represents negligible hazard requiring no precautions is the opposite of its intended meaning. This pictogram is unrelated to purely environmental hazards, which have their own separate symbol. It is also not limited only to long-term storage conditions.
  211. The correct answer is A (A risk of infection to humans or to animals from exposure to the discarded material): the biohazardous infectious materials pictogram identifies substances or organisms capable of causing disease. It is unrelated to fire, static discharge, cryogenic hazards, or noise, which are addressed by entirely different symbols or safety measures. This pictogram signals the need for infection control precautions such as PPE and proper containment. It commonly appears on biohazard bags and specimen containers.
  212. The correct answer is A (Section 4, the standardized section that specifically addresses recommended first aid measures.): in the standardized sixteen-section Safety Data Sheet format, Section 4 is designated for first aid measures specific to that chemical. Section 9 covers physical and chemical properties, not first aid. Section 14 addresses transport information, and Section 1 covers product and supplier identification. Knowing this standardized layout helps staff quickly locate critical information during an emergency.
  213. The correct answer is B (Review the reagent's Safety Data Sheet for detailed hazard and handling information first.): checking the SDS before first use ensures the worker understands the chemical's hazards, required PPE, and emergency procedures. Assuming safety based on appearance or relying on informal advice can miss critical hazard information. WHMIS labels and SDS documentation are mandatory, not optional, for hazardous products. This proactive review is a basic expectation of safe chemical handling.
  214. The correct answer is B (Chemicals should be segregated by compatibility class.): storing chemicals by compatibility, such as separating oxidizers from flammables, prevents accidental mixing that could cause fire, explosion, or toxic gas release. Alphabetical or size-based storage ignores chemical hazard interactions entirely. Storing incompatible substances like acids and bases together is a serious safety violation. Compatibility-based storage is a core chemical safety principle in every laboratory.
  215. The correct answer is D (Upright, secured storage prevents tipping, which could otherwise damage the valve and release gas suddenly.): an upright, secured cylinder is far less likely to fall and shear off its valve, which could otherwise create an uncontrolled and forceful gas release. This has nothing to do with label readability or WHMIS compliance specifically. Evaporation concerns relate to cryogenic liquids, not compressed gas cylinder orientation. Proper securing is a fundamental compressed gas safety requirement.
  216. The correct answer is A (Severe cold burns to exposed skin combined with a genuine risk of asphyxiation in the room.): liquid nitrogen can cause cryogenic burns on contact and, because it displaces oxygen as it evaporates, poses an asphyxiation risk in enclosed or poorly ventilated areas. It carries no inherent radiation hazard. Electrical shock is not a typical hazard associated with cryogenic storage tanks. Biohazard exposure may be a separate concern for the specimen itself, but it does not describe the cryogenic hazard specifically.
  217. The correct answer is C (Cryogenic-rated insulated gloves worn together with a protective face shield during retrieval.): these are specifically designed to protect against extreme cold contact and potential splash from liquid nitrogen. Standard exam gloves and sterile surgical gloves do not provide adequate insulation against cryogenic temperatures. Working without gloves at all would risk immediate cold burns. Proper cryogenic PPE is essential whenever handling frozen storage systems.
  218. The correct answer is A (A frayed and visibly damaged electrical cord discovered attached to a centrifuge in the department): this is a classic example of a physical hazard, since it involves a mechanical or environmental risk rather than a chemical or biological one. Spilled acid is a chemical hazard, and an improperly labelled biohazard bag relates to biological hazard communication. An expired reagent is a quality concern rather than a physical safety hazard. Recognizing hazard categories helps staff apply the correct control measures.
  219. The correct answer is B (Stop use, disconnect power if it is safe to do so, and report the hazard immediately.): sparking indicates a serious electrical fault that could cause shock or fire, so the equipment must be taken out of service immediately and reported. Continuing to work near it, even while avoiding direct contact, still leaves a fire and shock risk present. Taping the outlet does not address the underlying electrical fault. Prompt reporting ensures qualified personnel can inspect and repair the hazard.
  220. The correct answer is A (Noise-induced hearing loss resulting from prolonged, unprotected exposure to loud laboratory equipment): prolonged exposure to high noise levels from laboratory equipment can gradually damage hearing over time if unprotected. This exposure is unrelated to chemical burns, radiation sickness, or cryogenic frostbite, which come from entirely different hazard types. Hearing protection and noise monitoring help reduce this specific occupational risk. Recognizing noise as a genuine physical hazard supports appropriate protective measures.
  221. The correct answer is D (Securing the cylinder upright away from the sink, using a proper strap or chain restraint.): the gas cylinder pictogram identifies the container as holding gas under pressure, and pressurized cylinders must be secured upright and kept clear of water sources to prevent tipping, valve damage, or corrosion. Leaving it unsecured risks the cylinder falling and damaging the valve. Placing it under a dripping faucet exposes the valve and fittings to unnecessary moisture and corrosion. Storing it on its side under a drainpipe combines both an unsafe orientation and water exposure.
  222. The correct answer is A (Ionizing radiation carries enough energy to remove electrons from atoms, potentially damaging cells directly.): this defines the key distinction, since ionizing radiation has enough energy to alter atomic structure and cause biological damage, unlike non-ionizing radiation. Saying non-ionizing radiation is always more hazardous reverses the actual relationship. Both types can be detected by appropriate monitoring devices, so that claim is false. Non-ionizing radiation is also found outside laboratory settings, such as in sunlight and radio waves.
  223. The correct answer is B (Ultraviolet light emitted from the germicidal lamp inside a biological safety cabinet.): UV light is a form of non-ionizing radiation commonly encountered in laboratory settings, such as UV sterilization lamps in safety cabinets. Gamma rays, alpha particles, and beta particles are all forms of ionizing radiation associated with radioactive decay. Distinguishing between these types helps staff apply the correct protective measures for each. UV exposure still requires eye and skin protection despite being non-ionizing.
  224. The correct answer is C (It is used to monitor the wearer's cumulative radiation exposure accumulated over a defined time period): a dosimeter badge tracks the amount of radiation a worker has been exposed to across a defined monitoring period, helping ensure exposure stays within safe limits. It has no function related to temperature, biological pathogens, or noise. Regular badge review allows early identification of rising exposure trends. This monitoring is a key part of a radiation safety program.
  225. The correct answer is A (Avoid ingestion, inhalation, and skin contact, and use appropriate PPE and a fume hood.): the skull and crossbones pictogram identifies the chemical as acutely toxic, meaning even a small exposure through swallowing, breathing, or skin contact can cause serious harm, so appropriate PPE and engineering controls like a fume hood are required. Handling it without gloves ignores the exposure route the pictogram warns about. Eye-level storage is a convenience choice unrelated to toxicity and does not address the actual hazard. An opened or partially used container does not become less toxic.
  226. The correct answer is B (Proper ergonomic setup reduces the risk of musculoskeletal injury from repetitive strain and poor posture.): proper ergonomic setup helps prevent injuries like repetitive strain and back or neck problems that can develop from prolonged, poorly positioned work. This has a real, measurable effect on staff health, contrary to claiming no impact. Ergonomics is equally relevant to laboratory bench work, not just office settings. Addressing ergonomic risk is now an explicit competency expectation for laboratory staff.
  227. The correct answer is B (Using proper wrist positioning, taking scheduled breaks, and adjusting overall equipment height correctly.): these ergonomic adjustments directly reduce the repetitive strain associated with prolonged pipetting. Increasing speed would likely worsen the strain rather than help it. Switching to a heavier pipette adds more physical load rather than reducing it. Thicker gloves do not address the underlying wrist positioning problem causing the discomfort.
  228. The correct answer is A (Positioning the eyepieces and chair height to maintain a neutral neck and back posture): adjusting the microscope and seating to keep the spine and neck in a neutral position reduces strain during extended use. Placing the microscope at maximum height regardless of the user's position ignores individual ergonomic needs. Standing continuously without any seated option can create its own fatigue-related risks. Ergonomic setup should be tailored to the individual worker's body and task.
  229. The correct answer is C (To decontaminate the waste by destroying microorganisms using pressurized steam heat treatment.): the autoclave uses high-pressure steam at elevated temperature to kill microorganisms in biohazardous waste before disposal. It is not primarily intended for volume compaction, plastic recycling, or drying liquid waste. Its central purpose is microbial destruction to render waste safe for standard disposal pathways. Proper cycle time and temperature are essential for effective decontamination.
  230. The correct answer is A (They confirm the sterilization cycle reached an adequate temperature and time to kill microorganisms.): indicator tape and biological indicators provide visual or biological confirmation that the autoclave cycle achieved effective sterilization conditions. They are not used to label bag contents, measure weight, or track shipping destinations. This verification step is a critical quality check, since color change alone does not always guarantee complete sterilization. Regular biological indicator testing supports ongoing confidence in the autoclave's performance.
  231. The correct answer is C (Disposed of through regular municipal waste streams, kept separate from any biohazardous waste.): general waste like clean paper and packaging poses no biological or chemical hazard and can be discarded through standard waste streams. Mixing it with sharps containers or biohazardous waste is unnecessary and wasteful. Autoclaving is reserved for waste that requires decontamination, not general waste. Flushing waste down the sink is never an appropriate disposal method regardless of waste type.
  232. The correct answer is C (A dedicated biohazardous waste stream following institutional and government disposal regulations.): biomedical waste such as sharps and specimen containers must be segregated into a properly labelled biohazardous waste pathway to prevent exposure and comply with regulations. Mixing it with recyclable paper or general garbage would expose waste handlers to unnecessary risk. Indefinite storage without a disposal plan is not an acceptable practice. Proper segregation protects both laboratory staff and downstream waste handlers.
  233. The correct answer is C (Chemical waste, following the institution's established hazardous chemical disposal procedures for reagents.): expired formalin is a hazardous chemical and must be disposed of through the designated chemical waste pathway rather than general or biomedical waste. Placing it in general waste would expose handlers and the environment to a toxic chemical. It does not belong in the sharps or biomedical waste stream either, since its hazard is chemical, not biological. Following the institution's chemical waste protocol ensures safe and compliant disposal.
  234. The correct answer is D (Storage for a defined decay period until the material's radioactivity drops to a safe level.): many radioactive waste materials, especially those with short half-lives, are stored until their activity decays to a level considered safe for standard disposal. Immediate disposal in general waste would violate radiation safety regulations. Mixing it with chemical waste ignores its distinct regulatory handling requirements. Radioactive materials always require special handling, never treatment as ordinary waste.
  235. The correct answer is D (Materials that cannot withstand or are otherwise inappropriate for standard autoclave heat treatment methods.): chemical decontamination is used when heat treatment is not suitable, such as for certain heat-sensitive or chemically incompatible biohazardous materials. It is not the default method for all sharps waste, which typically follows a defined sharps disposal pathway. It also does not apply to radioactive materials or general office waste, which have their own separate disposal routes. Selecting the correct treatment method depends on the specific waste characteristics.
  236. The correct answer is A (A red or orange colored bag clearly marked with the standard biohazard symbol printed on it): this standardized colour and symbol combination clearly identifies biohazardous waste and distinguishes it from general or recyclable waste. A plain white or clear bag would fail to communicate the hazard to anyone handling it. Blue bags are not the standard designation for chemical waste in most facilities. Clear visual identification of biohazard bags is essential for correct waste segregation.
  237. The correct answer is C (Don appropriate PPE, contain the leak, and follow the institutional spill and biohazard protocol.): a leaking specimen presents a biological hazard that requires proper PPE and containment before any further handling occurs. Discarding only the outer bag while continuing to process the leaking tube ignores the ongoing contamination risk. Resealing and sending the leaking container onward could expose downstream staff. Wiping a leak with a bare paper towel provides no protection to the worker's skin.
  238. The correct answer is A (Alert others nearby, don appropriate PPE, and contain the spill before applying disinfectant.): this sequence ensures nearby staff are warned, the responder is protected, and the spill is physically contained before chemical decontamination begins. Using plain water alone does not adequately disinfect a biological spill. Leaving a spill uncovered until end of shift allows continued contamination risk. Applying disinfectant before donning PPE exposes the responder to the hazard unnecessarily.
  239. The correct answer is B (To determine the correct neutralizing agent and required personal protective equipment for that chemical.): the Safety Data Sheet provides chemical-specific guidance on safe cleanup methods, neutralizing agents, and protective equipment needed for that particular hazard. Purchase price and marketing details are unrelated to spill response safety. Saying SDS review is not relevant to spill response contradicts standard chemical safety practice. Consulting it first helps avoid using an incompatible or ineffective cleanup method.
  240. The correct answer is B (Restricting the area, notifying radiation safety personnel, and monitoring the area for contamination.): a radioactive spill requires specialized response steps beyond a standard chemical spill, including area restriction and involvement of trained radiation safety staff. A standard chemical spill kit alone is not adequate for radioactive contamination. Allowing staff to continue working immediately after wiping the area ignores the need for contamination monitoring. Disposing of radioactive waste in general waste would violate radiation safety regulations.
  241. The correct answer is B (The pictogram signals a chronic health hazard, so exposure should be minimized using proper engineering controls.): the health hazard pictogram identifies chemicals linked to long-term effects such as carcinogenicity or organ toxicity, so exposure should be reduced using controls like fume hoods and appropriate PPE. It does not describe only immediately fatal exposures, that concern belongs to the acute toxicity pictogram instead. Saying no engineering controls are needed ignores the entire purpose of this warning symbol. Repeated skin contact without protection directly contradicts what this pictogram is warning staff about.
  242. The correct answer is A (Wash the wound thoroughly with soap and water immediately and report the incident right away): immediate washing helps reduce the risk of infection, and prompt reporting allows timely risk assessment and any needed follow-up testing. Finishing the current task first unnecessarily delays critical first aid. A bandage alone without washing and reporting does not address either the wound care or the exposure risk assessment. Waiting until the end of shift significantly delays time-sensitive follow-up care.
  243. The correct answer is C (It allows for timely risk assessment, potential source-patient testing, and appropriate post-exposure follow-up.): prompt reporting enables the institution to evaluate exposure risk and arrange any necessary testing or prophylaxis quickly, which can be time-sensitive. Saying it has no impact on medical follow-up ignores this critical time-sensitive process. Reporting is important regardless of whether bleeding is visible. It also serves clinical, not just statistical, purposes.
  244. The correct answer is B (Wash the exposed area thoroughly with soap and water and follow institutional exposure protocol.): the biohazard pictogram identifies the specimen as potentially infectious, so even with intact skin the area should be washed thoroughly and the exposure reported through the institution's protocol. Hand sanitizer alone does not replace thorough washing for this type of exposure. Taking no action ignores the potential for pathogen transfer despite intact skin. Covering the area with a glove does nothing to decontaminate the skin underneath.
  245. The correct answer is C (Flushing the affected area with copious amounts of water for the recommended duration.): immediate and prolonged flushing helps dilute and remove the chemical from the skin, minimizing tissue damage. Applying a neutralizing chemical directly to skin can cause an additional reaction and is not recommended first aid. Wrapping the area tightly before flushing traps the chemical against the skin. Waiting for symptoms before acting delays critical early intervention.
  246. The correct answer is B (Cool the affected area with cool running water and seek further medical care as needed.): cool running water helps reduce tissue damage from a thermal burn and further medical evaluation can be sought if needed. Applying ice directly can cause additional tissue damage and is not recommended. A chemical neutralizer is inappropriate for a thermal, non-chemical burn. Vigorous rubbing can worsen burn injury and should be avoided.
  247. The correct answer is B (Avoid direct and prolonged skin contact, and wear gloves while using this irritant product.): the exclamation mark pictogram identifies the product as an irritant, meaning it can cause skin or eye irritation, so gloves should be worn and prolonged skin contact avoided. This pictogram has nothing to do with fire hazards, that warning belongs to the flame pictogram. Eye protection matters too, but skin contact is not irrelevant given the irritant classification. Dilution can reduce concentration but does not automatically eliminate the irritant hazard the label is warning about.
  248. The correct answer is C (Decontaminate the affected area and notify radiation safety personnel promptly following the exposure.): a suspected radioisotope exposure requires prompt decontamination and involvement of radiation safety staff to assess and manage the exposure appropriately. Ignoring it because there is no immediate discomfort could allow a real exposure to go unaddressed. Applying a bandage without washing first fails to remove contamination. Waiting twenty-four hours significantly delays time-sensitive radiation safety response.
  249. The correct answer is A (Assessing for serious injury and calling for appropriate help before moving them unnecessarily): moving an injured person without first assessing for serious injury, such as a possible spinal injury, can worsen the outcome. Immediately repositioning them without assessment ignores this risk. Resuming work and waiting to see if it worsens delays needed care. Asking them to walk it off dismisses the need for proper assessment entirely.
  250. The correct answer is A (Approximately one hundred to one hundred twenty compressions delivered per minute during CPR.): current basic life support guidelines recommend this compression rate range for effective adult CPR. Rates as low as forty to sixty or as high as two hundred to two hundred twenty per minute would be far outside recommended and effective ranges. Maintaining the correct rate helps ensure adequate blood circulation during resuscitation. This rate is a core element taught in basic CPR certification.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Check responsiveness and call for emergency help or activate the emergency response system.): confirming unresponsiveness and getting help on the way are essential first steps before beginning compressions. Starting compressions without any assessment could be inappropriate if the person is actually responsive. A blood pressure cuff is not a priority in an unresponsive emergency requiring possible CPR. Waiting for a supervisor before acting could dangerously delay life-saving care.
  2. The correct answer is B (Compressions should reach a depth of approximately five to six centimeters into the chest.): this compression depth range is recommended for effective adult CPR to adequately circulate blood during cardiac arrest. A depth of only half a centimeter would be far too shallow to be effective. Fifteen centimeters would be excessive and could cause injury. Saying depth does not matter contradicts established CPR guidelines, since both rate and depth are critical for effectiveness.
  3. The correct answer is B (It delivers a controlled electrical shock that may help restore a normal heart rhythm.): an automated external defibrillator analyzes the heart rhythm and, if appropriate, delivers a shock to help restore normal electrical activity during certain types of cardiac arrest. It does not measure oxygen levels or administer medication. It also does not replace the need for chest compressions, which should continue as directed by the device. AEDs are designed for use by trained laypersons and staff as part of the overall CPR response.
  4. The correct answer is D (Preparing a volatile organic solvent for a chemistry assay is best performed inside a fume hood.): a fume hood is appropriate here because it protects against inhalation of chemical vapours during solvent handling. Processing an aerosol-generating infectious specimen requires a biological safety cabinet, not a fume hood. Storing sharps containers and charging batteries have no relationship to either piece of equipment. Selecting the right containment device depends on whether the hazard is chemical or biological.
  5. The correct answer is A (The nearby emergency shower, designed to flush large areas of skin and clothing quickly.): a significant acid splash covering skin and clothing requires immediate, large-volume flushing best provided by an emergency shower. A fire extinguisher addresses fire, not chemical exposure. A first aid kit's bandages do not address active chemical contamination. Applying spill kit absorbent granules directly to skin would be inappropriate and could worsen the injury.
  6. The correct answer is D (A fume hood protects against chemical vapours, while a biological safety cabinet protects against aerosols.): this correctly distinguishes their different primary hazard focuses and containment designs. Saying they are functionally identical ignores real differences in filtration and airflow design. A biological safety cabinet is not a chemical storage device. A fume hood does not provide UV sterilization as its primary function. Choosing the correct cabinet type depends on the specific hazard being managed.
  7. The correct answer is C (A chain or strap that securely fastens the cylinder firmly to the transport cart itself.): this restraint prevents the cylinder from tipping or falling during transport, protecting the valve from damage. A locking wheel mechanism alone does not address the risk of the cylinder itself falling off the cart. A padded top for stacking equipment is unrelated to cylinder safety and could add instability. An open frame without restraint fails to secure the cylinder at all.
  8. The correct answer is D (Certain chemical spills, such as strong acids, genuinely require specific neutralization before safe cleanup.): some chemicals require targeted neutralizing agents to safely reduce their hazard level before physical cleanup can proceed. Water alone may not adequately neutralize or may even react dangerously with certain chemicals. Saying neutralizing agents are purely decorative misrepresents their functional safety role. Spill kits are specifically designed and stocked for chemical incident response.
  9. The correct answer is C (Applying standard precautions.): routine practices apply to every patient encounter because infection status is often unknown, so gloves and hand hygiene remain required. Skipping gloves based on assumed low risk contradicts the entire principle of standard precautions. Applying airborne precautions to every patient by default is excessive and not standard practice. Precaution use should not depend on patient request but on established infection control policy.
  10. The correct answer is C (Severe skin and eye damage from this chemical's strongly corrosive properties.): the corrosion pictogram warns that concentrated sulfuric acid can cause severe skin burns and eye damage on contact, requiring protective equipment such as goggles and acid-resistant gloves. Sulfuric acid is not flammable or biohazardous, so that combination does not describe its actual hazard. It is a liquid, not an explosive or a compressed gas, so those hazard descriptions do not apply here either. The corrosion pictogram specifically flags contact damage as the primary concern for handling this chemical.
  11. The correct answer is A (So they can quickly and correctly alert others and initiate evacuation during a fire emergency): knowing the pull station's location and activation procedure allows staff to respond quickly and effectively during an actual fire. Saying this knowledge is only relevant to maintenance staff ignores every worker's role in emergency response. Pull stations are functional safety devices, not decorative fixtures. Familiarity with their location directly affects how quickly an evacuation can begin.
  12. The correct answer is A (Elevators can malfunction or become trapped during a fire and are considered unsafe to use.): elevators can stop functioning or become unsafe during a fire due to power loss or shaft conditions, so stairs are the safer evacuation route. Stairs are not simply preferred for speed under normal conditions, but specifically for safety during emergencies. This rule is not limited to staff carrying equipment. It reflects a well established life safety principle in fire evacuation planning.
  13. The correct answer is A (Positioning cords away from water sources and regularly inspecting them for any visible damage.): keeping cords away from moisture and checking them for wear reduces the risk of electrical shock or fire near heat-generating equipment. Running cords through puddles or overloading outlets significantly increases hazard risk. Ignoring visible cord damage simply because the device still functions overlooks a real safety concern. Regular inspection and safe cord placement are basic electrical safety practices.
  14. The correct answer is C (Collect the leftover liquid in a designated hazardous waste container for proper disposal.): the environment pictogram warns that the chemical is harmful to aquatic life and ecosystems, so it must be collected in designated hazardous waste containers rather than released into drains or general trash. Pouring it down the sink drain sends the hazard directly into the water system, which the pictogram specifically warns against. Regular trash disposal does not meet hazardous waste handling requirements for this chemical. Leaving it in an open, unlabeled container creates both an exposure and a spill risk.
  15. The correct answer is C (The exposure should be reviewed and work practices reassessed to reduce further radiation exposure.): reaching an action level should prompt a review of the worker's practices and possible adjustments to reduce future radiation exposure. Ignoring the reading unless symptoms appear disregards the preventive purpose of monitoring. Simply replacing the badge without investigation misses the underlying cause of elevated exposure. Permanent reassignment without any review is an extreme response that skips proper investigation.
  16. The correct answer is D (Shielding, meaning the use of lead or other dense material barriers to absorb or block radiation.): shielding involves placing dense material between the worker and the radiation source to absorb or block exposure. Increasing time near the source directly increases exposure rather than reducing it. Removing monitoring badges eliminates an important safety check rather than providing shielding. Cardboard offers negligible shielding and would not be an appropriate storage material for isotopes.
  17. The correct answer is D (Store the container away from flammable and organic materials, since oxidizers intensify combustion.): the flame-over-circle pictogram identifies the product as an oxidizer, meaning it can intensify a fire if it contacts flammable or organic materials, so it should be stored separately from those substances. Placing it beside unrelated cleaning products without separation ignores this reactive risk. A sharps disposal area is intended for used needles and lancets, not chemical storage. Storing the container upside down does nothing to address the actual chemical hazard the pictogram describes.
  18. The correct answer is C (Biohazardous culture plates and other contaminated disposable labware generated during routine testing.): these items require decontamination before disposal, making the steam autoclave the appropriate treatment method. Non-contaminated paperwork does not require autoclave treatment and belongs in general waste. Flammable solvent should never be placed in an autoclave, since heat and pressure with a flammable liquid create a serious hazard. A sealed radioactive source requires specialized radioactive waste handling, not autoclaving.
  19. The correct answer is A (Isolate the area, don appropriate PPE, and follow the spill and biohazard response protocol): a leaking biohazard bag requires containment and proper protective equipment before any cleanup begins, following the institution's established protocol. Leaving it in place without action allows continued contamination risk. Kicking the bag aside could cause further leakage or spread contamination. Picking it up with bare hands directly exposes the worker to a biological hazard.
  20. The correct answer is C (Avoid disturbing or moving the bottle, and notify the safety officer immediately for proper handling.): the exploding bomb pictogram warns that the chemical can be explosive, and a dried-out reagent that originally required liquid stabilization can become shock-sensitive, so the safety officer must be notified rather than handling it casually. Continuing to use it as normal ignores the explosive risk the pictogram is specifically warning about. Moving it to a busier area increases the number of people exposed if it were to detonate. Shaking the bottle to test it could trigger the exact hazard the label is warning against.
  21. The correct answer is C (Used sharps must be disposed of immediately into a designated puncture-resistant sharps container.): this is the correct, safe standard for sharps disposal to prevent injury to anyone handling the item afterward. Recapping needles by hand, even carefully, remains a recognized cause of needlestick injuries. Using a sharps container past its fill line increases puncture risk rather than reducing it. Sharps disposal requirements apply to all sharp items, including lancets, not only needles.
  22. The correct answer is B (To ensure that someone else remains aware and able to respond quickly if an emergency occurs.): a buddy system or notification protocol provides a safety net so help can be summoned quickly if something goes wrong while working alone with hazards. This is a genuine safety measure, not merely a scheduling formality. It does not exist to reduce staffing numbers on a shift. It also has no connection to tracking lunch breaks.
  23. The correct answer is C (Practicing correct fire extinguisher use together with correct eyewash station response procedures.): these skills can be safely practiced and assessed through simulation without creating actual danger, consistent with common safety training approaches. Real-time handling of an active hemorrhage or an actual radioactive spill involves genuine danger that simulation training is meant to avoid recreating unsafely. A real needlestick injury should never be intentionally caused for training purposes. Simulation allows repeated, low-risk practice of critical emergency response skills.
  24. The correct answer is B (Storing strong chemical oxidizers physically separate from flammable materials elsewhere in the cabinet.): keeping incompatible chemical classes apart, such as oxidizers and flammables, prevents dangerous reactions or increased fire risk if they were to mix or come into contact. Storing all liquids together regardless of class ignores known chemical compatibility hazards. Storing acids and bases together unlabelled is a serious safety violation that can cause dangerous reactions. Storage decisions should be based on hazard compatibility, not container size alone.
  25. The correct answer is D (To limit the spread of potentially infectious aerosols and control who enters the controlled area.): restricted access and closed doors in a containment level 2 area help contain aerosols and ensure only authorized, appropriately trained personnel enter during procedures. This practice is not primarily about air conditioning or noise reduction. Restricted access is a core, deliberate biosafety control measure, not an unrelated administrative rule. Controlling entry directly supports the containment goals of the biosafety level.
  26. The correct answer is C (The chemical's official Safety Data Sheet, kept on file for every hazardous product in the department.): the SDS provides authoritative, chemical-specific information on required ventilation and handling precautions. An unverified internet search or personal odor-based judgment is not a reliable safety source. A container's decorative label design provides no meaningful safety information either. Consulting the SDS is the standard and expected practice before working with an unfamiliar chemical.
  27. The correct answer is A (A fitted N95 respirator, together with a protective gown, gloves, and eye protection.): this combination reflects the additional precautions needed for a highly infectious airborne pathogen, layering respiratory protection on top of standard barrier PPE. Gloves alone would leave the respiratory and other exposure routes unprotected. A surgical mask without gloves or a gown provides inadequate overall protection. Saying no PPE is needed contradicts the entire purpose of additional precautions for higher-risk pathogens.
  28. The correct answer is B (Open-toed footwear provides inadequate protection against dropped sharps or accidentally spilled chemicals.): closed-toe shoes protect the feet from injury caused by dropped sharp objects or spilled hazardous liquids in specimen processing areas. This rule exists for genuine safety reasons, not merely aesthetics. Footwear does directly affect injury risk in a laboratory setting. This requirement applies to everyday laboratory work, not only during fire drills.

Ignoring the spill until routine cleaning time leaves a serious hazard unaddressed for an unacceptable period. Reopening the area to normal traffic immediately would expose more people to contamination. Skipping documentation after cleanup fails to meet standard incident reporting requirements for a biohazard event.

Unlocked, unlabelled storage of agents would violate containment level 2 practices entirely.

Waiting for verbal confirmation before acting can dangerously delay a required evacuation.

  36. Saying propping the door open has no effect ignores the cabinet's core safety design purpose. The cabinet's function is fire containment, not dust control. Leaving it open does not meaningfully improve ventilation in a way that offsets the added fire risk.

Certification requirements are not exclusive to fume hoods, since biological safety cabinets have their own certification standards. Every position must have a matching counterweight directly opposite it. Running an unbalanced centrifuge can damage the rotor bearings and is a safety hazard. Stopping the run and rebalancing the tubes solves the vibration immediately.

Blowing it out would actually add extra volume beyond the calibrated amount. TC pipettes work the opposite way and require rinsing to deliver their full contents. Recognizing the marking on the pipette barrel tells you which technique to use.

  99.   100. The correct answer is B (Dehydrating and infiltrating tissue so it can later be embedded and sectioned.): a tissue processor moves specimens through a programmed sequence of solutions that dehydrate and infiltrate the tissue, preparing it for embedding and later sectioning.
